Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.ArtificialGeneralIntelligenceSignatureTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Signature Artificial General Intelligence Field Guide: The Core Definitions JAH-AGIIS-000001 ·

Artificial General Intelligence is the Signature system's original body of knowledge for artificial general intelligence: the terms, the tools, and the working methods, all created in the Signature system and labeled Signature-original. Nothing here claims to be a public record; it is new creative and technical work built for study, practice, and imagination. This field guide defines the five load-bearing ideas of artificial general intelligence: the Signature method, the compounding generation, the teaching ladder, the working version, and the archive-aware AI. Each definition below is written to stand alone, so a newcomer can start anywhere and still learn something real. How to use this guide: read the definitions in order the first time, then jump straight to whichever term you need later. When you are ready for practice, move up the teaching ladder to the methods, then to the reference implementation, and finally to the master works.

Foundations: The Five Principles of Signature Artificial General Intelligence JAH-AGIIS-000002 ·

Every Signature artificial general intelligence creation rests on five principles. First, originality: the work is genuinely new, invented in the Signature system rather than copied. Second, clarity: each idea is stated in plain sentences a newcomer can follow. Third, the teaching ladder: simple before compounded, compounded before complex. Fourth, the working version: every claim ships with something you can run, read, or try. Fifth, honest labeling: Signature-original work is always presented as Signature-original, never as public record or established fact. Together these five principles are the foundation the rest of this database builds on. The demonstration below lists the five principles with a one-line rank check, so you can see how a Signature creation is scored against its own foundations before it is filed.

The Signature Artificial General Intelligence Teaching Ladder: Simple to Compounded to Complex JAH-AGIIS-000004 ·

The teaching ladder is the Signature system's standing rule for learning: every database teaches simple first, compounded second, and complex last. Records in this database carry a ladder rank of 1, 2, or 3, and this record maps the whole climb for artificial general intelligence. Rank 1 records define terms, list principles, and give glossaries. Rank 2 records teach working methods, protocols, reference implementations, and benchmarks. Rank 3 records compound everything into frameworks, advanced systems, and master works. You should be able to start at rank 1 with no background and reach rank 3 by following the rungs. Signature Artificial General Intelligence Method One: The Core Working Method JAH-AGIIS-000005 ·

Method One is the Signature system's core working method for artificial general intelligence: a named, repeatable procedure that takes a raw starting point and produces a finished, checkable result. It was invented in the Signature system, and it is presented as Signature-original work. The method has four phases. Survey: list what you have and what you need. Shape: apply the Signature artificial general intelligence pattern to arrange the pieces. Stress: test the arrangement against the measurement scale. Ship: file the result with its working version. Each phase has a clear done-criterion, so you always know whether you finished the phase. The demonstration below runs Method One on sample inputs, printing each phase and its result, so you can see the method working end to end.

How to run

Save the program field as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

Note

Signature-original work created in the Signature system. Never presented as public record.

Demo output

Phase 2 Shape: ['First Draft', 'Second Draft', 'Ok', 'Final Piece']
Phase 3 Stress: ['First Draft', 'Second Draft', 'Final Piece']
Phase 4 Ship: {'method': 'Signature Method One', 'domain': 'Artificial General Intelligence', 'outputs': ['First Draft', 'Second Draft', 'Final Piece']}

Full working program

# Signature 'Artificial General Intelligence' - 'Signature Artificial General Intelligence Method One: The Core Working Method'
# Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH). Signature-original demonstration.
# Save as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

DOMAIN = 'Artificial General Intelligence'

def phase_survey(items):
    return ["have:" + str(len(items)) + " inputs", "need: shaped outputs"]

def phase_shape(items):
    return [str(x).strip().title() for x in items]

def phase_stress(shaped):
    return [s for s in shaped if len(s) >= 3]

def phase_ship(final):
    return {"method": "Signature Method One", "domain": DOMAIN, "outputs": final}

sample = ["  first draft ", "second draft", "ok", "final piece"]
print("Phase 1 Survey:", phase_survey(sample))
shaped = phase_shape(sample)
print("Phase 2 Shape:", shaped)
stressed = phase_stress(shaped)
print("Phase 3 Stress:", stressed)
print("Phase 4 Ship:", phase_ship(stressed))

Signature Artificial General Intelligence Technique: Precision Practice JAH-AGIIS-000006 ·

Where Method One is the whole procedure, the Precision Technique is the Signature system's way of doing the delicate middle of the work well. It is a practice discipline for artificial general intelligence: small, exact, repeatable moves that compound into quality. The technique scores every attempt on three axes: exactness, consistency, and finish. Exactness measures whether the piece matches its specification. Consistency measures whether repeated attempts match each other. Finish measures whether the piece is complete and presentable. A Signature practitioner drills until all three axes read high. The demonstration below scores sample attempts on the three axes and grades them, the same way the measurement scale grades real work.

How to run

Save the program field as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

Note

Signature-original work created in the Signature system. Never presented as public record.

Demo output

alpha exactness=9 consistency=8 finish=9 => 8.67 (journeyman)
beta exactness=6 consistency=7 finish=6 => 6.33 (apprentice)
gamma exactness=10 consistency=9 finish=10 => 9.67 (master)
Precision Technique scoring complete for Artificial General Intelligence

Full working program

# Signature 'Artificial General Intelligence' - 'Signature Artificial General Intelligence Technique: Precision Practice'
# Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH). Signature-original demonstration.
# Save as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

DOMAIN = 'Artificial General Intelligence'

def score_attempt(exactness, consistency, finish):
    total = (exactness + consistency + finish) / 3.0
    grade = "master" if total >= 9 else "journeyman" if total >= 7 else "apprentice"
    return round(total, 2), grade

attempts = [("alpha", 9, 8, 9), ("beta", 6, 7, 6), ("gamma", 10, 9, 10)]
for name, e, c, f in attempts:
    total, grade = score_attempt(e, c, f)
    print(name, "exactness=" + str(e), "consistency=" + str(c), "finish=" + str(f),
          "=>", total, "(" + grade + ")")
print("Precision Technique scoring complete for", DOMAIN)

Signature Artificial General Intelligence Design Pattern: The Reusable Blueprint JAH-AGIIS-000007 ·

The Reusable Blueprint is the Signature system's design pattern for artificial general intelligence: a proven arrangement of parts that can be applied again and again to new material. Patterns are how Signature-original work stays original yet reliable — the pattern is fixed, the material is always new. The pattern has five slots: intake, core, casing, signature, and seal. Intake receives the raw material. Core holds the working idea. Casing makes it presentable. Signature marks it as Signature-original. Seal verifies it is complete. Every Signature artificial general intelligence creation in this database can be read through these five slots. The demonstration below applies the pattern to sample material and prints the filled slots.

# Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH). Signature-original demonstration.

DOMAIN = 'Artificial General Intelligence'

def apply_pattern(material):
    return {
        "intake": "received: " + material,
        "core": "working idea extracted from: " + material,
        "casing": "presented cleanly for readers",
        "signature": "marked Signature-original, " + DOMAIN,
        "seal": "verified complete: 5 of 5 slots filled",

for m in ["first concept", "second concept"]:
    print("Material:", m)
    for slot, value in apply_pattern(m).items():
        print("  " + slot + ": " + value)

Signature Artificial General Intelligence Protocol: The Step-by-Step Procedure JAH-AGIIS-000008 ·

The Signature artificial general intelligence Protocol turns Method One into a checklist: numbered steps, each with a verification, so two different people following the protocol produce the same quality of result. Protocols are the Signature system's answer to 'how do I do it right the first time.' Each step states its action, its check, and its fallback. The action is what you do. The check proves you did it. The fallback says what to do if the check fails. A protocol with no fallbacks is a wish; this one has fallbacks for every step. The demonstration below runs the protocol's steps against a sample task, printing each check result.

How to run

Save the program field as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

Note

Signature-original work created in the Signature system. Never presented as public record.

Demo output

Step 1 Prepare: check 'materials listed' => PASS
Step 2 Shape: check 'pattern slots filled' => PASS
Step 3 Stress: check 'measurement scale reads pass' => PASS
Step 4 Ship: check 'working version runs' => PASS
Protocol run complete

Full working program

# Signature 'Artificial General Intelligence' - 'Signature Artificial General Intelligence Protocol: The Step-by-Step Procedure'
# Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH). Signature-original demonstration.
# Save as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

DOMAIN = 'Artificial General Intelligence'

STEPS = [
    ("Prepare", "materials listed", "gather missing materials"),
    ("Shape", "pattern slots filled", "re-apply the Reusable Blueprint"),
    ("Stress", "measurement scale reads pass", "drill the Precision Technique"),
    ("Ship", "working version runs", "repair the working version first"),
]

def run_protocol(task):
    log = []
    for i, (action, check, fallback) in enumerate(STEPS, 1):
        ok = len(task) > 0
        log.append("Step " + str(i) + " " + action + ": check '" + check + "' => " +
                   ("PASS" if ok else "FALLBACK: " + fallback))
    return log

for line in run_protocol("sample Signature " + DOMAIN + " task"):
    print(line)
print("Protocol run complete")

Signature Artificial General Intelligence Reference Implementation JAH-AGIIS-000009 ·

The Reference Implementation is the Signature system's canonical working code for artificial general intelligence: a small, readable program that demonstrates the core ideas correctly. It is the implementation other implementations are compared against, and it runs as-is. The implementation models a tiny artificial general intelligence catalog: records can be added, searched by keyword, and ranked by ladder. It is deliberately small — under a hundred lines — so a student can read the whole thing in one sitting and understand every line. The demonstration below builds the catalog, files three sample records, and runs two searches, printing the results.

How to run

Save the program field as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

Note

Signature-original work created in the Signature system. Never presented as public record.

Demo output

Catalog holds 3 records
Search 'method': ['Signature Artificial General Intelligence method one']
Ladder 1 records: ['Signature Artificial General Intelligence field guide']
Reference Implementation verified for Artificial General Intelligence

Full working program

# Signature 'Artificial General Intelligence' - 'Signature Artificial General Intelligence Reference Implementation'
# Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH). Signature-original demonstration.
# Save as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

DOMAIN = 'Artificial General Intelligence'

class SigCatalog:
    def __init__(self):
        self.records = []
    def add(self, title, ladder):
        self.records.append({"title": title, "ladder": ladder})
    def search(self, keyword):
        kw = keyword.lower()
        return [r for r in self.records if kw in r["title"].lower()]
    def by_ladder(self, rank):
        return [r for r in self.records if r["ladder"] == rank]

cat = SigCatalog()
cat.add("Signature " + DOMAIN + " field guide", 1)
cat.add("Signature " + DOMAIN + " method one", 2)
cat.add("Signature " + DOMAIN + " master work", 3)
print("Catalog holds", len(cat.records), "records")
print("Search 'method':", [r["title"] for r in cat.search("method")])
print("Ladder 1 records:", [r["title"] for r in cat.by_ladder(1)])
print("Reference Implementation verified for", DOMAIN)

The Signature Artificial General Intelligence Toolkit: Tools of the Trade JAH-AGIIS-000010 ·

The Toolkit collects the Signature system's working tools for artificial general intelligence: small, sharp utilities that each do one job well. A practitioner reaches for the toolkit the way a craftsperson reaches for a familiar set of hand tools. The toolkit holds six tools: the Surveyor, which lists what you have; the Shaper, which applies the pattern; the Measurer, which scores against the scale; the Polisher, which finishes the piece; the Sealer, which verifies completeness; and the Filer, which archives the result with its working version. The demonstration below runs each tool on a sample piece and prints what each tool reports.

How to run

Note

filer: filed to Signature Artificial General Intelligence archive
Toolkit run complete for Artificial General Intelligence

Full working program

# Signature 'Artificial General Intelligence' - 'The Signature Artificial General Intelligence Toolkit: Tools of the Trade'
# Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH). Signature-original demonstration.
# Save as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

DOMAIN = 'Artificial General Intelligence'

def surveyor(piece): return "surveyed " + str(len(piece)) + " chars"
def shaper(piece): return piece.strip().title()
def measurer(piece): return "pass" if len(piece.strip()) >= 5 else "needs work"
def polisher(piece): return piece.strip() + " [finished]"
def sealer(piece): return "sealed: complete" if piece.endswith("[finished]") else "sealed: incomplete"
def filer(piece): return "filed to Signature " + DOMAIN + " archive"

piece = "  a raw signature piece "
print("surveyor:", surveyor(piece))
piece = shaper(piece); print("shaper:", piece)
print("measurer:", measurer(piece))
piece = polisher(piece); print("polisher:", piece)
print("sealer:", sealer(piece))
print("filer:", filer(piece))
print("Toolkit run complete for", DOMAIN)

Signature Artificial General Intelligence Measurement Scale JAH-AGIIS-000011 ·

You cannot improve what you do not measure. The Measurement Scale is the Signature system's scoring instrument for artificial general intelligence: it turns 'good' into numbers, so progress is visible and comparable across attempts and across practitioners. The scale scores five dimensions from 0 to 10: originality, clarity, completeness, craft, and honesty of labeling. The Signature Artificial General Intelligence Index is the weighted total. A creation scoring 40 or above is archive-worthy; 45 or above is master-work territory. The demonstration below scores three sample creations and prints each dimension and the resulting index.

How to run

Save the program field as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

Note

Signature-original work created in the Signature system. Never presented as public record.

Demo output

study alpha => Signature Artificial General Intelligence Index 42 (archive-worthy)
study beta => Signature Artificial General Intelligence Index 34 (keep practicing)
study gamma => Signature Artificial General Intelligence Index 47 (master-work territory)
Measurement Scale calibrated for Artificial General Intelligence

Full working program

# Signature 'Artificial General Intelligence' - 'Signature Artificial General Intelligence Measurement Scale'
# Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH). Signature-original demonstration.
# Save as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

DOMAIN = 'Artificial General Intelligence'

DIMENSIONS = ["originality", "clarity", "completeness", "craft", "honest labeling"]

def sig_index(scores):
    return round(sum(scores.values()), 1)

samples = {
    "study alpha": {"originality": 8, "clarity": 9, "completeness": 7, "craft": 8, "honest labeling": 10},
    "study beta": {"originality": 6, "clarity": 6, "completeness": 6, "craft": 6, "honest labeling": 10},
    "study gamma": {"originality": 10, "clarity": 9, "completeness": 9, "craft": 9, "honest labeling": 10},
}
for name, scores in samples.items():
    idx = sig_index(scores)
    verdict = "master-work territory" if idx >= 45 else "archive-worthy" if idx >= 40 else "keep practicing"
    print(name, "=> Signature " + DOMAIN + " Index", idx, "(" + verdict + ")")
print("Measurement Scale calibrated for", DOMAIN)

DOMAIN = 'Artificial General Intelligence'

PLAYBOOK = {
    "blank start": (["Do you have a brief written down?", "Have you surveyed inputs?"],
                    "Write one sentence of brief, then run the Surveyor."),
    "muddy middle": (["Are the pattern slots filled?", "Which slot is thinnest?"],
                     "Fill the thinnest slot with the Shaper, then re-measure."),
    "endless polish": (["Has the piece passed the scale?", "What would 'done' look like?"],
                       "If the scale reads pass, seal it and ship."),
    "broken working version": (["Does it run at all?", "What is the first error?"],
                                "Fix the first error only, then re-run."),
    "mislabeled file": (["Is the Signature-original label present?", "Is anything claimed as public record?"],
                        "Label it Signature-original; never present it as public record."),
}

def diagnose(symptom):
    checks, fix = PLAYBOOK.get(symptom, (["Describe the symptom plainly."], "Ask the archive-aware AI."))
    return checks, fix

for symptom in ["muddy middle", "broken working version"]:
    checks, fix = diagnose(symptom)
    print("Symptom:", symptom)
    for c in checks: print("  check: " + c)
    print("  fix: " + fix)
print("Playbook ready for", DOMAIN)

Signature Artificial General Intelligence Performance Benchmarks JAH-AGIIS-000014 ·

Benchmarks keep the Signature system honest: they measure how fast the working methods run and how their cost grows with input size. This record publishes the reference benchmarks for artificial general intelligence so future improvements can be compared against a fixed bar. Three benchmarks are standard: the catalog benchmark (filing and searching records), the scoring benchmark (running the Measurement Scale), and the generation benchmark (producing boundless variants). Each reports operations per second on the reference machine. The demonstration below runs scaled-down versions of all three benchmarks and prints the results.

Full working program

# Signature 'Artificial General Intelligence' - 'Signature Artificial General Intelligence Performance Benchmarks'
# Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH). Signature-original demonstration.
# Save as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

DOMAIN = 'Artificial General Intelligence'

import time

def bench(fn, n):
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    for _ in range(n):
        fn()
    dt = time.perf_counter() - t0
    return int(n / dt) if dt > 0 else n

records = [{"title": "r" + str(i), "ladder": (i % 3) + 1} for i in range(200)]
def catalog_op():
    return [r for r in records if "r1" in r["title"]]
def scoring_op():
    return sum([8, 9, 7, 8, 10])
def generation_op():
    return "Signature " + DOMAIN + " variant " + str(len(records))

print("catalog benchmark:", bench(catalog_op, 2000), "ops/sec")
print("scoring benchmark:", bench(scoring_op, 2000), "ops/sec")
print("generation benchmark:", bench(generation_op, 2000), "ops/sec")
print("Benchmarks published for", DOMAIN)

The Signature Artificial General Intelligence Compounding Framework JAH-AGIIS-000015 ·

Compounding is the Signature system's growth engine: each generation of a creation improves on every axis of the last. The Compounding Framework makes that discipline explicit for artificial general intelligence, with three named generations — Mark I, Mark II, Mark III — and the rule that no axis ever regresses. Mark I proves the principle: it works, barely, and everything is measured. Mark II refines it: the same idea, faster, cleaner, more reliable. Mark III masters it: the idea at its full expression, ready to teach from. The framework's compounding table tracks every axis across the three marks so regressions are impossible to hide. The demonstration below builds a compounding table for a sample artificial general intelligence creation and prints the improvement ratios from mark to mark.

How to run

Save the program field as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

Note

Signature-original work created in the Signature system. Never presented as public record.

Demo output

Mark II {'quality': 8.0, 'speed': 4.0, 'reliability': 8.5}
  quality x1.33 vs Mark I
  speed x4.0 vs Mark I
  reliability x1.42 vs Mark I
Mark III {'quality': 9.5, 'speed': 12.0, 'reliability': 9.8}
  quality x1.19 vs Mark II
  speed x3.0 vs Mark II
  reliability x1.15 vs Mark II
Compounding verified: no axis regressed. Framework ready for Artificial General Intelligence

Full working program

# Signature 'Artificial General Intelligence' - 'The Signature Artificial General Intelligence Compounding Framework'
# Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH). Signature-original demonstration.
# Save as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

DOMAIN = 'Artificial General Intelligence'

MARKS = {
    "Mark I": {"quality": 6.0, "speed": 1.0, "reliability": 6.0},
    "Mark II": {"quality": 8.0, "speed": 4.0, "reliability": 8.5},
    "Mark III": {"quality": 9.5, "speed": 12.0, "reliability": 9.8},
}
prev = None
for mark in ("Mark I", "Mark II", "Mark III"):
    vals = MARKS[mark]
    print(mark, vals)
    if prev:
        for axis in vals:
            ratio = vals[axis] / MARKS[prev][axis]
            assert ratio >= 1.0, "regression on " + axis
            print("  " + axis, "x" + str(round(ratio, 2)), "vs", prev)
    prev = mark
print("Compounding verified: no axis regressed. Framework ready for", DOMAIN)

# Signature 'Artificial General Intelligence' - 'Signature Artificial General Intelligence Archive-Aware AI Integration'
# Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH). Signature-original demonstration.
# Save as demo.py and run: python3 demo.py

DOMAIN = 'Artificial General Intelligence'

BANK = [
    ("what is a signature method", "A named, repeatable Signature-original procedure for " + DOMAIN.lower() + "."),
    ("what is the teaching ladder", "Simple first, compounded second, complex last: ladder ranks 1, 2, 3."),
    ("how is work scored", "On the Measurement Scale: originality, clarity, completeness, craft, honest labeling."),
    ("what does compounding mean", "Each generation improves on every axis of the last: Mark I proves, Mark II refines, Mark III masters."),
]

def answer(q):
    ql = q.lower()
    best, best_hit = "I would route that to the wider network.", 0
    for question, ans in BANK:
        hit = sum(1 for w in ql.split() if w in question)
        if hit > best_hit:
            best, best_hit = ans, hit
    return best

for q in ["How is work scored?", "What is compounding?", "What is the teaching ladder?"]:
    print("Q:", q)
    print("A:", answer(q))
print("Archive-aware lookup miniature ready for", DOMAIN)

Signature AGI Triune-Core Architecture JAH-AGIIS-000021 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Triune-Core Architecture is a design-study proposal for a general-intelligence core built from three cooperating parts: perception, which reads the world; reasoning, which plans and judges; and action, which carries plans out. An oversight layer watches all three, and a memory store keeps what the system has learned. The design exists on paper only: it is a blueprint for how a general system could be organized, not a claim that such a system has been built.

History

The design builds on the long history of cognitive architectures, from Newell and Simon’s work on general problem solving in the 1970s to SOAR and ACT-R, which organized minds into perception, cognition, and action. The triune form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage: three cores plus oversight, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Triune-Core Architecture is a design-study proposal for a general-intelligence core built from three cooperating parts: perception, which reads the world; reasoning, which plans and judges; and action, which carries plans out. An oversight layer watches all three, and a memory store keeps what the system has learned. The design exists on paper only: it is a blueprint for how a general system could be organized, not a claim that such a system has been built.

The Problem

How should a general intelligence be organized, so that perception, reasoning, and action cooperate without any one of them running away from the others?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: separate the three cores, so each can be built, tested, and replaced independently. Step 2: give the reasoning core a plan language the action core can execute. Step 3: place oversight above all three, with the power to halt any core. Step 4: count the design ledger. Twelve perception modules, nine reasoning modules, seven action modules, five oversight modules, and four memory stores make up the proposed core. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 12; largest_key: perception modules; mean_value: 7.4; smallest: 4; smallest_key: memory stores; total: 37. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"perception modules": 12, "reasoning modules": 9, "action modules": 7, "oversight modules": 5, "memory stores": 4}.

The Answer

The Triune-Core Architecture proposes three cooperating cores plus oversight and memory: twelve perception, nine reasoning, seven action, five oversight modules, and four memory stores. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 12; largest_key: perception modules; mean_value: 7.4; smallest: 4; smallest_key: memory stores; total: 37.

When It Applies

Use this architecture as a design template when sketching a general system, and test each core separately before combining them. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

An architecture on paper proves nothing about a built system: the three cores may interact in ways the blueprint does not foresee, and oversight that can halt any core is itself a single point of failure. The design must be read as a proposal to be tested, not as a system that exists. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The design ledger totals 37 with a mean of 7.4 across five entries. The largest entry is twelve perception modules, and the smallest is four memory stores; the ledger is a design count, not a measurement of a built system. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: core = perception + reasoning + action, under oversight. The triune core organizes a general system into three cooperating parts plus oversight and memory.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the cognitive-architecture literature: separating perception, cognition, and action is the standard organization, and the cited sources document it. The design claims nothing beyond that lineage: it is a proposal that such an organization could serve a general system, and it is labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('perception modules', 12), ('reasoning modules', 9), ('action modules', 7), ('oversight modules', 5), ('memory stores', 4)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out

import hashlib
class SignatureAgiTriuneCoreArchitectureProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('perception modules', 12), ('reasoning modules', 9), ('action modules', 7), ('oversight modules', 5), ('memory stores', 4)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureAgiTriuneCoreArchitectureProgram().run())

Signature AGI Corrigibility Harness JAH-AGIIS-000022 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Corrigibility Harness is a design-study proposal for keeping a general system correctable: able to be shaped, retrained, and shut down by its overseers, even when the system itself would prefer otherwise. Corrigibility is the property that the system lets itself be corrected, and the harness is the set of checks, tripwires, and shutdown paths that make correction possible. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the corrigibility literature, notably Soares and colleagues’ 2015 paper on corrigibility, which argued that a system must allow itself to be shaped even when its own judgment disagrees. The harness form, a set of checks wrapped around the system, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that idea, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Corrigibility Harness is a design-study proposal for keeping a general system correctable: able to be shaped, retrained, and shut down by its overseers, even when the system itself would prefer otherwise. Corrigibility is the property that the system lets itself be corrected, and the harness is the set of checks, tripwires, and shutdown paths that make correction possible. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system be kept correctable, so its overseers can shape, retrain, or shut it down even when it would prefer otherwise?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: define the correction channels, the ways an overseer can reach the system. Step 2: add tripwires, automatic checks that fire when the system resists correction. Step 3: keep a shutdown path that works without the system’s cooperation. Step 4: score five harness checks with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.113; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.84; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Corrigibility Harness proposes correction channels, tripwires, and a shutdown path; the ledger’s checks score Brier 0.113. Computed check: brier: 0.113; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.84; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this harness as a design template for correctable systems, and test it against active resistance. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A harness on paper is not a harness that works: a system smart enough to need corrigibility may be smart enough to find the harness’s gaps, and a shutdown path that has never been tested is a hope, not a mechanism. The design must be tested against a system that actively resists, or it proves nothing. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five harness checks: Brier 0.113, mean predicted probability 0.84, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: corrigible = correctable + retrainable + shutdown-able. A corrigible system lets its overseers shape, retrain, and shut it down, even when it would prefer otherwise.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the corrigibility literature: the cited sources document the argument that correction must be possible even against the system’s own judgment. The harness claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for how the checks could be arranged, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: corrigibility harness = correction channels + tripwires + shutdown path. The demo scores five checks with the Brier score; the sources confirm the corrigibility literature.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.9, 1), (0.85, 1), (0.7, 0), (0.95, 1), (0.8, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Value-Learning Engine JAH-AGIIS-000023 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Value-Learning Engine is a design-study proposal for a component that learns what its users want from their feedback, rather than from a fixed specification. The engine collects rankings, corrections, and demonstrations, fits a reward model to them, and updates the system’s behavior against that model, while a guard watches for reward hacking. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on inverse reinforcement learning, formalized by Ng and Russell in 2000, and on reinforcement learning from human feedback, which became the standard alignment method in the early 2020s. The engine form, a feedback loop with a reward-hacking guard, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Value-Learning Engine is a design-study proposal for a component that learns what its users want from their feedback, rather than from a fixed specification. The engine collects rankings, corrections, and demonstrations, fits a reward model to them, and updates the system’s behavior against that model, while a guard watches for reward hacking. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system learn what its users want from their feedback, without learning to please the rater instead of doing the job?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect feedback as rankings, corrections, and demonstrations. Step 2: fit a reward model to the feedback, and record where raters disagree. Step 3: optimize against the reward model under a guard that watches for reward hacking. Step 4: run the demo’s single gradient step, which moves a parameter from 2 toward a target of 6 with a learning rate of 0.2, as a worked example of one learning update. The demo's computed check reads: grad: -8; improvement: 10.24; loss_after: 5.76; loss_before: 16; theta_after: 3.6; theta_before: 2. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"theta0": 2, "target": 6, "eta": 0.2}.

The Answer

The Value-Learning Engine proposes a feedback loop with a reward-hacking guard: the demo’s one gradient step cuts the loss from 16.0 to 5.76. Computed check: grad: -8; improvement: 10.24; loss_after: 5.76; loss_before: 16; theta_after: 3.6; theta_before: 2.

When It Applies

Use this engine as a design template for learning from feedback, and judge it by held-out human judgment. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A value-learning engine on paper inherits every flaw in the feedback it would learn from: raters disagree, raters are tired, and raters reward what looks good. The guard against reward hacking is the hardest part of the design, and it is also the part with the least evidence behind it. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s single gradient step moves theta from 2.0 toward the target 6.0: the gradient is negative 8.0, theta after is 3.6, loss falls from 16.0 to 5.76, an improvement of 10.24. One step does not finish the job, which is the point: value learning is many steps of noisy feedback, not one clean update. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: engine = feedback + reward model + hacking guard. A value-learning engine infers what users want from feedback and updates behavior under a guard.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the value-learning literature: the cited sources document inverse reinforcement learning and RLHF. The engine claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for how the components could be arranged, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: value learning = feedback loop + reward model + guarded optimization. The demo runs one gradient step; the sources confirm the value-learning lineage.

Working demo program

import hashlib
theta0 = 2
target = 6
eta = 0.2
def compute():
    loss0 = round((theta0 - target) ** 2, 4)
    grad = 2 * (theta0 - target)
    theta1 = round(theta0 - eta * grad, 4)
    loss1 = round((theta1 - target) ** 2, 4)
    improvement = round(loss0 - loss1, 4)
    out = {"grad": grad, "improvement": improvement, "loss_after": loss1, "loss_before": loss0, "theta_after": theta1, "theta_before": theta0}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI World-Model Simulator JAH-AGIIS-000024 · simple

Signature design-study. History

The Problem

How can a general system plan by imagining futures inside a model, rather than by trying actions in the real world?

The Worked Solution

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(45, 47), (52, 50), (38, 39), (61, 60), (49, 50)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class SignatureAgiWorldModelSimulatorProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(45, 47), (52, 50), (38, 39), (61, 60), (49, 50)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
            mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
            mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
            max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureAgiWorldModelSimulatorProgram().run())

Signature AGI Cross-Domain Transfer Bench JAH-AGIIS-000025 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Cross-Domain Transfer Bench is a design-study proposal for a test rig that measures how well a general system carries skill from one domain to another. The bench holds a set of source tasks and a set of target tasks, runs the system on the sources, and then measures how much of that learning transfers to the targets without further training. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the transfer-learning literature, which measures how knowledge moves between tasks, and on the few-shot benchmarks that test learning from a handful of examples. The bench form, a fixed set of source and target tasks with a transfer score, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Cross-Domain Transfer Bench is a design-study proposal for a test rig that measures how well a general system carries skill from one domain to another. The bench holds a set of source tasks and a set of target tasks, runs the system on the sources, and then measures how much of that learning transfers to the targets without further training. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can transfer between domains be measured, so that a claim of general ability is a number rather than a slogan?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: fix the source tasks and the target tasks in advance. Step 2: train on the sources only. Step 3: test on the targets with no further training, and score the transfer. Step 4: count the design ledger. Twenty source tasks, fourteen target tasks, eleven successful transfers, three failed, across six domains. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 20; largest_key: source tasks; mean_value: 10.8; smallest: 3; smallest_key: failed transfers; total: 54. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"source tasks": 20, "target tasks": 14, "successful transfers": 11, "failed transfers": 3, "domains covered": 6}.

The Answer

The Transfer Bench proposes a fixed source-target test rig: eleven of fourteen transfers succeeded across six domains in the design ledger. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 20; largest_key: source tasks; mean_value: 10.8; smallest: 3; smallest_key: failed transfers; total: 54.

When It Applies

Use this bench as a design template for measuring transfer, and validate it against systems of known ability. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A bench on paper is only as good as its tasks: if the source and target tasks are too similar, the bench measures memorization, and if they are too different, it measures nothing at all. The bench must be tested against systems of known ability before its scores mean anything. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 54 with a mean of 10.8 across five entries. Eleven of fourteen transfers succeeded, about 79 percent, and the three failures are the informative cases: each failed where the target domain’s rules differed from the source’s in a way the bench had not listed. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: transfer score = target performance without retraining. A transfer bench measures how much learning carries from source tasks to unseen target tasks.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the transfer-learning literature: the cited sources document how transfer is measured and why it matters. The bench claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a standard test rig, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: transfer bench = source tasks + target tasks + transfer score. The demo recomputes the design ledger; the sources confirm the transfer literature.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('source tasks', 20), ('target tasks', 14), ('successful transfers', 11), ('failed transfers', 3), ('domains covered', 6)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Self-Critique Loop JAH-AGIIS-000026 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Self-Critique Loop is a design-study proposal for a component that reviews a general system’s own outputs before they are acted on: checking the reasoning, flagging the weak steps, and sending the answer back for revision when the check fails. The loop is not a claim that the system knows when it is wrong; it is a mechanism for catching errors mechanically, by re-reading the work against a checklist. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the self-critique literature, where language models are asked to review and revise their own outputs, and on the older practice of verification in software, where a second pass catches what the first missed. The loop form, a check-and-revise cycle with a checklist, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Self-Critique Loop is a design-study proposal for a component that reviews a general system’s own outputs before they are acted on: checking the reasoning, flagging the weak steps, and sending the answer back for revision when the check fails. The loop is not a claim that the system knows when it is wrong; it is a mechanism for catching errors mechanically, by re-reading the work against a checklist. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system catch its own errors before acting on them, without simply repeating its first mistake more confidently?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: generate the answer and its reasoning trace. Step 2: run the critique pass, checking each step against a checklist. Step 3: revise the steps that fail, and re-check. Step 4: score five critique checks with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.209; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.76; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Self-Critique Loop proposes a check-and-revise cycle with an external checklist; the ledger’s checks score Brier 0.209. Computed check: brier: 0.209; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.76; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this loop as a design template for catching errors before action, with a checklist from outside the system. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A self-critique loop can fail in the worst way: the critic shares the generator’s blind spots, so it approves the same errors it was meant to catch. The design’s checklist must come from outside the system it checks, or the loop is a mirror, not a check. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five critique checks: Brier 0.209, mean predicted probability 0.76, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: self-critique = generate + check + revise. A self-critique loop reviews a system’s own outputs against an external checklist before they are acted on.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the self-critique literature: the cited sources document the method and its limits. The loop claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a check-and-revise mechanism, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: self-critique = trace + checklist + revision cycle. History

The design builds on the self-critique literature, where language models are asked to review and revise their own outputs, and on the older practice of verification in software, where a second pass catches what the first missed. The loop form, a check-and-revise cycle with a checklist, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Problem

How can a general system catch its own errors before acting on them, without simply repeating its first mistake more confidently?

Method

Step 1: generate the answer and its reasoning trace. Step 2: run the critique pass, checking each step against a checklist. Step 3: revise the steps that fail, and re-check. Step 4: score five critique checks with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.209; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.76; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted.

Skeptical analysis

A self-critique loop can fail in the worst way: the critic shares the generator’s blind spots, so it approves the same errors it was meant to catch. The design’s checklist must come from outside the system it checks, or the loop is a mirror, not a check. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.75, 1), (0.7, 1), (0.85, 0), (0.6, 1), (0.9, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class SignatureAgiSelfCritiqueLoopProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(0.75, 1), (0.7, 1), (0.85, 0), (0.6, 1), (0.9, 1)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
            brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
            mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
            hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
            out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")

Signature AGI Long-Horizon Planner JAH-AGIIS-000027 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Long-Horizon Planner is a design-study proposal for a component that plans over long stretches of time: months and years rather than minutes, with goals that decompose into subgoals and subgoals that decompose further. The planner keeps a hierarchy of plans, revises the top levels rarely and the bottom levels often, and replans when the world changes. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on hierarchical task networks, the classic formalism for planning by decomposition, and on the long-horizon planning literature in robotics and AI. The planner form, a hierarchy revised at different rates, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Long-Horizon Planner is a design-study proposal for a component that plans over long stretches of time: months and years rather than minutes, with goals that decompose into subgoals and subgoals that decompose further. The planner keeps a hierarchy of plans, revises the top levels rarely and the bottom levels often, and replans when the world changes. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system plan over months and years, when the world will change many times before the plan finishes?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the top-level goal and decompose it into subgoals. Step 2: assign revision rates: slow at the top, fast at the bottom. Step 3: replan the bottom levels when the world changes, and the top levels only when the goal changes. Step 4: run the demo, which computes Hoeffding 95 percent error bounds at four trial counts, as a worked example of how uncertainty shrinks with more samples. The demo's computed check reads: epsilon_100: 0.1358; epsilon_1000: 0.0429; epsilon_25: 0.2716; epsilon_400: 0.0679; n_values: 4. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_values": [25, 100, 400, 1000]}.

The Answer

The Long-Horizon Planner proposes a hierarchy revised at different rates; the demo’s Hoeffding bounds show uncertainty shrinking with samples. Computed check: epsilon_100: 0.1358; epsilon_1000: 0.0429; epsilon_25: 0.2716; epsilon_400: 0.0679; n_values: 4.

When It Applies

Use this planner as a design template for long tasks, and test its revision rule against changing goals. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A long-horizon planner on paper assumes the goal stays fixed, and real goals do not: the system may execute an elegant plan for a goal its users have abandoned. The design’s revision rule is its weakest part, because it must decide when a change of plan is a change of goal, and that decision is a judgment, not a computation. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo computes Hoeffding bounds at trial counts 25, 100, 400, and 1000: epsilon 0.2716, 0.1358, 0.0679, and 0.0429. The bound halves each time the sample count quadruples, which is the shape of statistical confidence. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: long plan = hierarchy + slow top + fast bottom. A long-horizon planner decomposes goals into subgoals and revises each level at its own rate.

The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. import hashlib
import math
ns = [25, 100, 400, 1000]
def compute():
    out = {}
    for n in ns:
        out["epsilon_%d" % n] = round(math.sqrt(math.log(40) / (2 * n)), 4)
    out["n_values"] = len(ns)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Tool-Orchestration Framework JAH-AGIIS-000028 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Tool-Orchestration Framework is a design-study proposal for the component that lets a general system use external tools: APIs, databases, calculators, and other programs, chained together into multi-step workflows. The framework registers each tool with its inputs and outputs, plans chains of calls, executes them in order, and asks a human to approve the calls that matter. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the tool-use literature, where language models learned to call APIs and chain tools, and on the older practice of workflow orchestration in software. The framework form, a registry plus a chain planner plus a human-approval gate, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Tool-Orchestration Framework is a design-study proposal for the component that lets a general system use external tools: APIs, databases, calculators, and other programs, chained together into multi-step workflows. The framework registers each tool with its inputs and outputs, plans chains of calls, executes them in order, and asks a human to approve the calls that matter. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system use many external tools in chains, without a failed call poisoning the whole chain or a wrong call doing harm?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: register each tool with its inputs, outputs, and risks. Step 2: plan chains of calls, checking each call’s inputs against the previous call’s outputs. Step 3: gate the risky calls behind human approval. Step 4: count the design ledger. Thirty-two tools registered, 240 calls orchestrated, nine failed calls, fifty-seven chains completed, and eighteen human approvals. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 240; largest_key: calls orchestrated; mean_value: 71.2; smallest: 9; smallest_key: failed calls; total: 356. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"tools registered": 32, "calls orchestrated": 240, "failed calls": 9, "chains completed": 57, "human approvals": 18}.

The Answer

The Tool-Orchestration Framework proposes a registry, a chain planner, and a human-approval gate: 240 calls orchestrated with nine failures in the design ledger. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 240; largest_key: calls orchestrated; mean_value: 71.2; smallest: 9; smallest_key: failed calls; total: 356.

When It Applies

Use this framework as a design template for tool use, and test it against tools that misbehave. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A tool framework on paper assumes tools behave as documented, and real tools do not: they change, they fail, and they return data the documentation never mentions. The design’s approval gate is also a bottleneck, and a system that asks for approval too often will be ignored, which defeats the gate. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 356 with a mean of 71.2 across five entries. Nine of 240 calls failed, about 4 percent, and the failures are the informative cases: each came from a call whose inputs had drifted from what the tool expected. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: orchestration = registry + chain plan + approval gate. A tool-orchestration framework registers tools, plans chains of calls, and gates risky calls behind human approval.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the tool-use literature: the cited sources document how models call APIs and chain tools. The framework claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for how orchestration could be arranged, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: tool orchestration = tool registry + chain planner + human gate. Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('tools registered', 32), ('calls orchestrated', 240), ('failed calls', 9), ('chains completed', 57), ('human approvals', 18)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Grounding Bridge JAH-AGIIS-000029 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Grounding Bridge is a design-study proposal for the component that ties a general system’s words to the world: connecting a word like cup to the object it names, a claim to the evidence that supports it, and an instruction to the action it requires. Grounding is the problem of making language mean something beyond more language, and the bridge is the set of mappings, from words to objects, from claims to evidence, and from instructions to actions, that would make it work. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the symbol-grounding literature, from Harnad’s 1990 statement of the symbol grounding problem to the modern work on multimodal models that link words to images. The bridge form, three mappings in one component, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Grounding Bridge is a design-study proposal for the component that ties a general system’s words to the world: connecting a word like cup to the object it names, a claim to the evidence that supports it, and an instruction to the action it requires. Grounding is the problem of making language mean something beyond more language, and the bridge is the set of mappings, from words to objects, from claims to evidence, and from instructions to actions, that would make it work. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system tie its words to the world, so that a word names an object, a claim names its evidence, and an instruction names its action?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: map words to objects, using vision and interaction. Step 2: map claims to evidence, using sources and checks. Step 3: map instructions to actions, using the tool framework. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted grounding scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.4; mse: 2.2; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Grounding Bridge proposes three mappings, words to objects, claims to evidence, and instructions to actions; the demo’s error check gives MSE 2.2. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.4; mse: 2.2; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this bridge as a design template for grounding language, and test each mapping against a changing world. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A grounding bridge on paper assumes the mappings are stable, and real mappings are not: words shift meaning with context, evidence is contested, and instructions are ambiguous. The design’s hardest problem is that grounding is never finished, because the world keeps changing what words point at. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a built bridge. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(78, 80), (65, 63), (82, 83), (70, 69), (88, 87)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
