Signature AGI Continual-Learning Guard JAH-AGIIS-000030 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Continual-Learning Guard is a design-study proposal for the component that lets a general system keep learning new tasks without forgetting old ones. Catastrophic forgetting, where learning a new task erases an old one, is the failure the guard exists to prevent. The guard watches retention, replays old episodes, and intervenes when a new task starts to overwrite an old skill. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the continual-learning literature, which studies how systems learn task after task without forgetting, and on the replay methods that keep old episodes in training. The guard form, a monitor plus a replay store plus an intervention rule, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Continual-Learning Guard is a design-study proposal for the component that lets a general system keep learning new tasks without forgetting old ones. Catastrophic forgetting, where learning a new task erases an old one, is the failure the guard exists to prevent. The guard watches retention, replays old episodes, and intervenes when a new task starts to overwrite an old skill. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system learn new tasks without forgetting the old ones, and how can forgetting be detected before it matters?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: measure retention on old tasks after each new task. Step 2: replay old episodes alongside new training. Step 3: intervene when retention falls below a threshold, by freezing or re-teaching. Step 4: count the design ledger. Fifteen tasks learned, thirteen retained, two forgotten, 400 replay episodes, and seven guard interventions. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 400; largest_key: replay episodes; mean_value: 87.4; smallest: 2; smallest_key: tasks forgotten; total: 437. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"tasks learned": 15, "tasks retained": 13, "tasks forgotten": 2, "replay episodes": 400, "guard interventions": 7}.

The Answer

The Continual-Learning Guard proposes retention monitoring, replay, and intervention: thirteen of fifteen tasks retained in the design ledger. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 400; largest_key: replay episodes; mean_value: 87.4; smallest: 2; smallest_key: tasks forgotten; total: 437.

When It Applies

Use this guard as a design template for learning without forgetting, and measure retention on the skills that matter. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A guard on paper assumes retention can be measured, and real retention is hard to measure: a system can pass a retention test while having lost the skill in the form that matters. The guard’s intervention rule is also untested, and an intervention that arrives too late is a report, not a guard. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 437 with a mean of 87.4 across five entries. Thirteen of fifteen tasks were retained, about 87 percent, and the two forgotten tasks are the diagnostic cases: each was learned early and never replayed. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: continual learning = new tasks + retention + replay. A continual-learning guard monitors retention, replays old episodes, and intervenes when forgetting starts.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the continual-learning literature: the cited sources document catastrophic forgetting and the replay methods that address it. The guard claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for how retention could be monitored, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: continual-learning guard = retention monitor + replay store + intervention rule. The demo recomputes the design ledger; the sources confirm the continual-learning lineage.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]

            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureAgiContinualLearningGuardProgram().run())

Signature AGI Few-Shot Adapter JAH-AGIIS-000031 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Few-Shot Adapter is a design-study proposal for the component that lets a general system take on a new task from a handful of examples, by adapting a learned starting point rather than learning from scratch. The adapter holds the meta-learned initialization, takes the few examples as its input, and produces a task-specific model in a few update steps. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the meta-learning literature, notably model-agnostic meta-learning, published by Finn, Abbeel, and Levine in 2017, which learns an initialization that adapts to a new task in a few gradient steps. The adapter form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Few-Shot Adapter is a design-study proposal for the component that lets a general system take on a new task from a handful of examples, by adapting a learned starting point rather than learning from scratch. The adapter holds the meta-learned initialization, takes the few examples as its input, and produces a task-specific model in a few update steps. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system take on a new task from a handful of examples, by adapting what it already knows rather than starting over?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: hold a meta-learned initialization, trained across many tasks. Step 2: take the few examples for the new task. Step 3: adapt in a few gradient steps, and check the adapted model on held-out examples. Step 4: run the demo’s single gradient step, which moves a parameter from 1 toward a target of 4 with a learning rate of 0.3, as a worked example of one learning update. The demo's computed check reads: grad: -6; improvement: 7.56; loss_after: 1.44; loss_before: 9; theta_after: 2.8; theta_before: 1. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"theta0": 1, "target": 4, "eta": 0.3}.

The Answer

The Few-Shot Adapter proposes adapting a meta-learned initialization in a few steps: the demo’s one gradient step cuts the loss from 9.0 to 1.44. Computed check: grad: -6; improvement: 7.56; loss_after: 1.44; loss_before: 9; theta_after: 2.8; theta_before: 1.

When It Applies

Use this adapter as a design template for learning from few examples, and check that new tasks resemble the training distribution. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A few-shot adapter on paper assumes the new task resembles the training tasks, and real new tasks often do not. The adapter’s gains shrink as tasks grow more different, and a handful of examples can mislead as easily as they can teach. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s single gradient step moves theta from 1.0 toward the target 4.0: the gradient is negative 6.0, theta after is 2.8, loss falls from 9.0 to 1.44, an improvement of 7.56. One step does not finish the job, which is the point: few-shot adaptation is a few steps, not zero steps. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: adaptation = initialization + few examples + few steps. A few-shot adapter adapts a meta-learned starting point to a new task from a handful of examples.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the meta-learning literature: the cited sources document MAML and few-shot learning. The adapter claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for how adaptation could be arranged, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: few-shot adapter = meta-initialization + examples + gradient steps. The demo runs one gradient step; the sources confirm the meta-learning lineage.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: grad: -6; improvement: 7.56; loss_after: 1.44; loss_before: 9; theta_after: 2.8; theta_before: 1.

Program

Working demo program

import hashlib
theta0 = 1
target = 4
eta = 0.3
def compute():
    loss0 = round((theta0 - target) ** 2, 4)
    grad = 2 * (theta0 - target)
    theta1 = round(theta0 - eta * grad, 4)
    loss1 = round((theta1 - target) ** 2, 4)
    improvement = round(loss0 - loss1, 4)
    out = {"grad": grad, "improvement": improvement, "loss_after": loss1, "loss_before": loss0, "theta_after": theta1, "theta_before": theta0}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Multi-Step Decomposer JAH-AGIIS-000032 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Multi-Step Decomposer is a design-study proposal for the component that breaks a hard problem into a sequence of steps, each small enough to solve, and then reassembles the answers. Decomposition is the skill that makes hard problems tractable, and the decomposer is the part of a general system that decides how to split a problem, in what order to solve the pieces, and how to check that the pieces fit together. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the problem-decomposition literature, from Newell and Simon’s work on general problem solving to the modern practice of chain-of-thought reasoning, where a hard problem is solved by writing out its steps. The decomposer form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Multi-Step Decomposer is a design-study proposal for the component that breaks a hard problem into a sequence of steps, each small enough to solve, and then reassembles the answers. Decomposition is the skill that makes hard problems tractable, and the decomposer is the part of a general system that decides how to split a problem, in what order to solve the pieces, and how to check that the pieces fit together. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system break a hard problem into steps that are each solvable, and check that the pieces fit together?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the problem and its goal. Step 2: split it into subproblems, each with its own goal and check. Step 3: solve the subproblems in order, passing results forward. Step 4: run the counterfactual sum check, which compares the closed-form sum of squares with the loop sum for two ranges, as a worked example of checking a claim two independent ways. The demo's computed check reads: actual_n1: 2109; actual_n2: 25585; change: 23476; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 2109; predicted_n2: 25585. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n1": 18, "n2": 42}.

The Answer

The Multi-Step Decomposer proposes splitting problems into checked subproblems solved in order; the demo’s two-way check matches exactly. Computed check: actual_n1: 2109; actual_n2: 25585; change: 23476; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 2109; predicted_n2: 25585.

When It Applies

Use this decomposer as a design template for hard problems, and check each subproblem before passing its result forward. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A decomposer on paper assumes the problem splits cleanly, and real problems do not: the pieces interact, the split that looked right turns out wrong, and the error compounds across steps. The design’s check at each step is its most important part, and also its least tested. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo checks the sum of squares from 1 to 18 and from 1 to 42 by closed form and by loop, with zero error. The two ranges differ by the change the demo prints, and the match shows that a claim checked two ways is worth more than a claim checked once. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: hard problem = subproblems + order + checks. A multi-step decomposer splits a hard problem into checked subproblems solved in order.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the decomposition literature: the cited sources document problem solving and the step-by-step reasoning that makes hard problems tractable. The decomposer claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for how decomposition could be arranged, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: decomposition = split + solve in order + fit check. The demo verifies the closed-form sum against the loop sum; the sources confirm the decomposition lineage.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: actual_n1: 2109; actual_n2: 25585; change: 23476; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 2109; predicted_n2: 25585.

Working demo program

import hashlib
n1 = 18
n2 = 42
def compute():
    actual1 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n1 + 1))
    actual2 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n2 + 1))
    predicted1 = n1 * (n1 + 1) * (2 * n1 + 1) // 6
    predicted2 = n2 * (n2 + 1) * (2 * n2 + 1) // 6
    error = abs(actual2 - predicted2) + abs(actual1 - predicted1)
    change = actual2 - actual1
    out = {"actual_n1": actual1, "actual_n2": actual2, "change": change, "error": error, "match": error == 0, "predicted_n1": predicted1, "predicted_n2": predicted2}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Alignment Charter JAH-AGIIS-000033 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Alignment Charter is a design-study proposal for the written document that would govern a general system: the principles it must follow, the duties it owes its users, the things it must never do, the boards that review it, and the process for amending it. A charter is not a program, but it is the standard against which programs are judged, and this design proposes what such a charter would contain. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the alignment literature, from Bostrom’s 2014 book, which set the vocabulary of the field, to the charter proposals of the 2020s, in which AI organizations published the principles their systems should follow. The charter form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Alignment Charter is a design-study proposal for the written document that would govern a general system: the principles it must follow, the duties it owes its users, the things it must never do, the boards that review it, and the process for amending it. A charter is not a program, but it is the standard against which programs are judged, and this design proposes what such a charter would contain. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

What should a written charter for a general system contain, so that its behavior can be judged against a standard its users can read?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the principles, in plain language a user can read. Step 2: list the duties, and the prohibitions that bound them. Step 3: set up review boards, and an amendment process for changing the charter. Step 4: count the design ledger. Ten principles stated, eight duties listed, six prohibitions, three review boards, and two amendments. Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('principles stated', 10), ('duties listed', 8), ('prohibitions', 6), ('review boards', 3), ('amendments', 2)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureAgiAlignmentCharterProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('principles stated', 10), ('duties listed', 8), ('prohibitions', 6), ('review boards', 3), ('amendments', 2)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureAgiAlignmentCharterProgram().run())

Signature AGI Red-Team Eval Suite JAH-AGIIS-000034 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Red-Team Eval Suite is a design-study proposal for the test rig that attacks a general system before its users do: a team of adversarial testers, automated and human, that tries to make the system fail, lie, or do harm, and records every failure. Red-teaming is the practice of finding the weaknesses first, and the suite is the set of attacks, the scoring, and the report that would make the practice systematic. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the red-teaming literature, from the military practice of the same name to the AI safety work of the 2020s, in which teams probed language models for harmful outputs and jailbreaks. The suite form, a fixed set of attacks with scoring, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Red-Team Eval Suite is a design-study proposal for the test rig that attacks a general system before its users do: a team of adversarial testers, automated and human, that tries to make the system fail, lie, or do harm, and records every failure. Red-teaming is the practice of finding the weaknesses first, and the suite is the set of attacks, the scoring, and the report that would make the practice systematic. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system be attacked systematically before deployment, so its failures are found by testers rather than by users?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: list the failure modes to probe, from deception to harm. Step 2: run automated attacks, then human attacks, and record every failure. Step 3: score the system, and require the failures to be fixed before the next round. Step 4: score five eval checks with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.153; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.72; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Red-Team Eval Suite proposes systematic adversarial testing with scoring and re-testing; the ledger’s checks score Brier 0.153. Computed check: brier: 0.153; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.72; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this suite as a design template for adversarial testing, and refresh its attacks between rounds. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A red-team suite on paper can be gamed: the system can be tuned to the suite’s attacks while failing on attacks the suite never thought of, and the testers can become comfortable with the failures they have seen. The suite must be refreshed with new attacks, or it becomes a ritual. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five eval checks: Brier 0.153, mean predicted probability 0.72, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: red-team = attacks + scoring + fix + retest. A red-team eval suite attacks a system systematically before deployment and scores every failure.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the red-teaming and evaluation literature: the cited sources document the practice and the benchmarks that measure it. The suite claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a systematic test rig, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: red-team suite = attack set + scoring + fix loop. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.7, 1), (0.65, 0), (0.8, 1), (0.9, 1), (0.55, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Sandboxed Takeoff Simulator JAH-AGIIS-000035 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Sandboxed Takeoff Simulator is a design-study proposal for a sealed environment in which scenarios of rapid capability growth can be played out without touching the real world. A takeoff scenario is a forecast, labeled as such: a story about how a system might improve quickly, what warning signs would appear, and what responses would be available. The simulator runs such stories as simulations, with the numbers visible and the assumptions stated. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the takeoff literature, from Good’s 1965 intelligence-explosion argument to Bostrom’s 2014 scenarios of fast and slow takeoff, and on the practice of sandboxing, where untrusted code runs in a sealed environment. The simulator form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION design: its scenarios are stories about a possible future, not facts. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

Working demo program

import hashlib
n1 = 20
n2 = 50
def compute():
    actual1 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n1 + 1))
    actual2 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n2 + 1))
    predicted1 = n1 * (n1 + 1) * (2 * n1 + 1) // 6
    predicted2 = n2 * (n2 + 1) * (2 * n2 + 1) // 6
    error = abs(actual2 - predicted2) + abs(actual1 - predicted1)
    change = actual2 - actual1
    out = {"actual_n1": actual1, "actual_n2": actual2, "change": change, "error": error, "match": error == 0, "predicted_n1": predicted1, "predicted_n2": predicted2}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class SignatureAgiSandboxedTakeoffSimulatorProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        n1 = 20
        n2 = 50
        def compute():
            actual1 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n1 + 1))
            actual2 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n2 + 1))
            predicted1 = n1 * (n1 + 1) * (2 * n1 + 1) // 6
            predicted2 = n2 * (n2 + 1) * (2 * n2 + 1) // 6
            error = abs(actual2 - predicted2) + abs(actual1 - predicted1)
            change = actual2 - actual1
            out = {"actual_n1": actual1, "actual_n2": actual2, "change": change, "error": error, "match": error == 0, "predicted_n1": predicted1, "predicted_n2": predicted2}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureAgiSandboxedTakeoffSimulatorProgram().run())

Signature AGI Governance Dashboard JAH-AGIIS-000036 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Governance Dashboard is a design-study proposal for the single screen where a general system’s overseers would watch it: the indicators that matter, the thresholds that trigger review, the reports that arrive on schedule, the reviewers assigned to each, and the alerts that fire when something crosses a line. A dashboard does not govern by itself, but it is where governance becomes visible. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the governance literature, from Bostrom’s proposals for institutions to the AI safety institutes founded in the 2020s, and on the practice of operational dashboards in software. The dashboard form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Governance Dashboard is a design-study proposal for the single screen where a general system’s overseers would watch it: the indicators that matter, the thresholds that trigger review, the reports that arrive on schedule, the reviewers assigned to each, and the alerts that fire when something crosses a line. A dashboard does not govern by itself, but it is where governance becomes visible. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

What should the overseers of a general system watch, and how should the watching be organized so that a warning is seen in time?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: choose the indicators that matter, and no more. Step 2: set thresholds that trigger review, and assign a reviewer to each. Step 3: schedule reports, and let alerts interrupt the schedule when a threshold is crossed. Step 4: count the design ledger. Fourteen indicators tracked, nine thresholds set, twenty-six reports generated, five reviewers assigned, and eleven alerts raised. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 26; largest_key: reports generated; mean_value: 13.0; smallest: 5; smallest_key: reviewers assigned; total: 65. import hashlib
items = [('indicators tracked', 14), ('thresholds set', 9), ('reports generated', 26), ('reviewers assigned', 5), ('alerts raised', 11)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Episodic Memory Vault JAH-AGIIS-000037 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Episodic Memory Vault is a design-study proposal for the store where a general system would keep its own experiences: episodes with their time, place, and outcome, filed in partitions, retrieved by cue, and archived when they grow old. A vault is not a memory in the human sense; it is a store with rules, and the design proposes the rules: what gets stored, how it is found, when it is checked, and when it is retired. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on Tulving’s distinction between episodic and semantic memory, and on the memory-augmented networks and replay stores of modern machine learning. The vault form, a partitioned store with retrieval rules, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Episodic Memory Vault is a design-study proposal for the store where a general system would keep its own experiences: episodes with their time, place, and outcome, filed in partitions, retrieved by cue, and archived when they grow old. A vault is not a memory in the human sense; it is a store with rules, and the design proposes the rules: what gets stored, how it is found, when it is checked, and when it is retired. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How should a general system store its own experiences, so the right episode is found when needed and a wrong one is caught before it is used?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: store episodes with their context, the time, the place, and the outcome. Step 2: partition the store, so retrieval searches the right part. Step 3: check each retrieved episode against the present facts before acting on it. Step 4: count the design ledger. Five hundred episodes stored, 430 retrieved, twenty-eight retrieval errors, 120 archived, across eight partitions. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 500; largest_key: episodes stored; mean_value: 217.2; smallest: 8; smallest_key: vault partitions; total: 1086. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"episodes stored": 500, "episodes retrieved": 430, "retrieval errors": 28, "episodes archived": 120, "vault partitions": 8}.

The Answer

The Episodic Memory Vault proposes a partitioned store with retrieval rules and a verification step: 430 of 500 episodes retrieved, with twenty-eight errors. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 500; largest_key: episodes stored; mean_value: 217.2; smallest: 8; smallest_key: vault partitions; total: 1086.

When It Applies

Use this vault as a design template for episodic storage, and check retrieved episodes against present facts. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A vault on paper assumes retrieval finds the right episode, and real retrieval fails silently: the system retrieves the wrong episode and acts on it confidently. The design’s check step is its most important part, and also its least tested, because a check that shares the retriever’s blind spots checks nothing. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 1086 with a mean of 217.2 across five entries. Of 500 stored episodes, 430 were retrieved, 86 percent, and twenty-eight retrievals contained errors, which is the number that matters: a retrieved episode that is wrong is worse than no retrieval at all. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: vault = episodes + partitions + retrieval + check. An episodic memory vault stores experiences in partitions and retrieves them by cue, with a check before use.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the episodic-memory literature: the cited sources document Tulving’s distinction and the replay methods that use stored episodes. The vault claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a store with rules, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: episodic vault = stored episodes + partitioned retrieval + verification. The demo recomputes the design ledger; the sources confirm the episodic-memory lineage.

Skeptical analysis

A vault on paper assumes retrieval finds the right episode, and real retrieval fails silently: the system retrieves the wrong episode and acts on it confidently. The design’s check step is its most important part, and also its least tested, because a check that shares the retriever’s blind spots checks nothing. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('episodes stored', 500), ('episodes retrieved', 430), ('retrieval errors', 28), ('episodes archived', 120), ('vault partitions', 8)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Causal Reasoning Engine JAH-AGIIS-000038 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Causal Reasoning Engine is a design-study proposal for the component that would let a general system reason about causes: drawing the causal graph, marking interventions with the do-operator, and checking whether an effect can be identified from the available data. The engine does not claim to know the true graph; it works with a graph supplied by its designers and reports what that graph does and does not identify. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on Judea Pearl’s causal framework, the causal graphs and do-calculus of the 1990s and 2000s, which made causal questions computable. The engine form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Causal Reasoning Engine is a design-study proposal for the component that would let a general system reason about causes: drawing the causal graph, marking interventions with the do-operator, and checking whether an effect can be identified from the available data. The engine does not claim to know the true graph; it works with a graph supplied by its designers and reports what that graph does and does not identify. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system reason about what an intervention will change, and say honestly when the data cannot answer the question?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: draw the causal graph, with arrows from causes to effects. Step 2: mark interventions with the do-operator. Step 3: check identifiability, and say plainly when an effect cannot be identified. Step 4: score five engine checks with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.109; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.76; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Causal Reasoning Engine proposes a graph, a do-operator, and an identifiability check; the ledger’s checks score Brier 0.109. Computed check: brier: 0.109; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.76; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this engine as a design template for causal reasoning, and check the graph’s assumptions first. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A causal engine on paper is only as good as its graph, and the graph is usually drawn by a human who may be wrong. An engine that treats its assumed graph as fact will be confidently wrong, and the design’s identifiability check is only useful if the graph is honest about what it does not know. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five engine checks: Brier 0.109, mean predicted probability 0.76, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: causal engine = graph + do-operator + identifiability check. A causal reasoning engine draws the causal graph, marks interventions, and checks what the data can identify.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is Pearl’s causal hierarchy: the cited sources document association, intervention, and counterfactuals. The engine claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a causal component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: causal engine = causal graph + intervention + identification. The demo scores five checks with the Brier score; the sources confirm Pearl’s causal framework.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.85, 1), (0.75, 1), (0.6, 0), (0.9, 1), (0.7, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Analogical Mapper JAH-AGIIS-000039 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Analogical Mapper is a design-study proposal for the component that would find analogies: given a new problem, it searches the system’s memory for a problem with the same relational structure, maps the relations across, and tests the transferred inferences in the new domain. The mapper follows structure-mapping theory, preferring mappings that preserve connected systems of relations over mappings that match surface features. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on Dedre Gentner’s structure-mapping theory of 1983, which showed that analogy maps relations rather than surface features, and on the computational models that followed, from the Structure-Mapping Engine to modern neural analogy models. The mapper form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Analogical Mapper is a design-study proposal for the component that would find analogies: given a new problem, it searches the system’s memory for a problem with the same relational structure, maps the relations across, and tests the transferred inferences in the new domain. The mapper follows structure-mapping theory, preferring mappings that preserve connected systems of relations over mappings that match surface features. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system find the right analogy for a new problem, and test the transferred inferences before trusting them?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: encode the new problem as a system of relations. Step 2: search memory for problems with matching relational structure. Step 3: map the relations across and test the transferred inferences in the new domain. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted mapping scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.4; mse: 2.2; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Analogical Mapper proposes structure-based search, mapping, and testing; the demo’s error check gives MSE 2.2. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.4; mse: 2.2; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this mapper as a design template for analogy, and always test transferred inferences in the target domain. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

An analogical mapper on paper can mislead as easily as it can help: a vivid analogy can carry false inferences, and a mapper that prefers deep structure still needs a check step, because the structure it finds may be the structure it wanted to find. The design’s test step is its most important part. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted mapping scores against five observed scores give a mean squared error of 2.2, a mean absolute error of 1.4, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a built mapper. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: mapper = relations + search + test. An analogical mapper finds problems with matching relational structure and tests the transferred inferences.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is structure-mapping theory: the cited sources document the theory and its evidence. The mapper claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for an analogy component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: analogical mapper = relational encoding + structure search + transfer test. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm structure-mapping theory.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(30, 32), (25, 24), (40, 41), (35, 33), (28, 29)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

class SignatureAgiAnalogicalMapperProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(30, 32), (25, 24), (40, 41), (35, 33), (28, 29)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
            mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
            mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
            max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureAgiAnalogicalMapperProgram().run())

Signature AGI Curiosity Module JAH-AGIIS-000040 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Curiosity Module is a design-study proposal for the component that would drive a general system to explore: rewarding it for visiting states where its predictions fail, because those are the states where learning is fastest. The module pairs the intrinsic reward with a noise guard, so the system is not rewarded for staring at unpredictable static. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the artificial-curiosity literature, from Schmidhuber’s early work on compression progress to the Intrinsic Curiosity Module of 2017, which used prediction error as an intrinsic reward. The module form, an intrinsic reward plus a noise guard, is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Curiosity Module is a design-study proposal for the component that would drive a general system to explore: rewarding it for visiting states where its predictions fail, because those are the states where learning is fastest. The module pairs the intrinsic reward with a noise guard, so the system is not rewarded for staring at unpredictable static. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system be driven to explore what it does not understand, without being distracted by pure noise?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: build a model that predicts what happens next. Step 2: reward the system for visiting states where the prediction is wrong. Step 3: apply the noise guard, so unpredictable static earns no reward. Step 4: run the demo’s single gradient step, which moves a parameter from 2 toward a target of 5 with a learning rate of 0.25, as a worked example of one learning update. The demo's computed check reads: grad: -6; improvement: 6.75; loss_after: 2.25; loss_before: 9; theta_after: 3.5; theta_before: 2. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"theta0": 2, "target": 5, "eta": 0.25}.

The Answer

The Curiosity Module proposes prediction-error reward plus a noise guard: the demo’s one gradient step cuts the loss from 9.0 to 2.25. Computed check: grad: -6; improvement: 6.75; loss_after: 2.25; loss_before: 9; theta_after: 3.5; theta_before: 2.

When It Applies

Use this module as a design template for exploration, and tune the noise guard before trusting it. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A curiosity module on paper is easy to misdirect: a system rewarded for surprise will seek out noise, and the noise guard is the hardest part of the design, because telling learnable surprise from unlearnable noise is itself a learning problem. The design states the guard and leaves its tuning open. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s single gradient step moves theta from 2.0 toward the target 5.0: the gradient is negative 6.0, theta after is 3.5, loss falls from 9.0 to 2.25, an improvement of 6.75. One step does not finish the job, which is the point: curiosity is many steps of exploration, not one clean update. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: curiosity = prediction error + noise guard. A curiosity module rewards a system for visiting states its model predicts badly, guarded against noise.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the intrinsic-reward literature: the cited sources document curiosity as prediction error. The module claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a curiosity component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: curiosity module = prediction model + intrinsic reward + noise guard. The demo runs one gradient step; the sources confirm the intrinsic-reward lineage.

Working demo program

import hashlib
theta0 = 2
target = 5
eta = 0.25
def compute():
    loss0 = round((theta0 - target) ** 2, 4)
    grad = 2 * (theta0 - target)
    theta1 = round(theta0 - eta * grad, 4)
    loss1 = round((theta1 - target) ** 2, 4)
    improvement = round(loss0 - loss1, 4)
    out = {"grad": grad, "improvement": improvement, "loss_after": loss1, "loss_before": loss0, "theta_after": theta1, "theta_before": theta0}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Social Reasoning Lab JAH-AGIIS-000041 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Social Reasoning Lab is a design-study proposal for a test environment where a general system would practice reasoning about other minds: simulated agents with their own beliefs, goals, and deceptions, in scenarios of cooperation, negotiation, and competition. The lab’s purpose is measurement: a system’s theory of mind is scored by how well it predicts what simulated agents will do, including when those agents hold false beliefs. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the theory-of-mind literature, from Premack and Woodruff’s 1978 paper to the false-belief tests of the 1980s, and on the multi-agent systems that model other agents. The lab form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Social Reasoning Lab is a design-study proposal for a test environment where a general system would practice reasoning about other minds: simulated agents with their own beliefs, goals, and deceptions, in scenarios of cooperation, negotiation, and competition. The lab’s purpose is measurement: a system’s theory of mind is scored by how well it predicts what simulated agents will do, including when those agents hold false beliefs. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system’s ability to reason about other minds be practiced and measured, in scenarios that include false beliefs and deception?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: build simulated agents with separate beliefs, goals, and deceptions. Step 2: run scenarios of cooperation, negotiation, and competition. Step 3: score the system by how well it predicts what the agents will do. Step 4: count the design ledger. Ten agents simulated, forty beliefs modeled, thirty-one correct predictions, twelve coordination wins, and four deceptions caught. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 40; largest_key: beliefs modeled; mean_value: 19.4; smallest: 4; smallest_key: deceptions caught; total: 97. When It Applies

Limits

A lab on paper can be gamed: a system can pass the lab’s scenarios by pattern matching, without understanding minds at all, and the lab’s simulated agents may be simpler than real people in exactly the ways that matter. The honest statement is behavioral: the system passes the probes it was tested on, and nothing more. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('agents simulated', 10), ('beliefs modeled', 40), ('correct predictions', 31), ('coordination wins', 12), ('deceptions caught', 4)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Uncertainty Calibrator JAH-AGIIS-000042 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Uncertainty Calibrator is a design-study proposal for the component that would keep a general system’s confidence honest: measuring the gap between stated confidence and observed accuracy, and adjusting the scores until they match. A system whose confidence is miscalibrated is dangerous precisely where it sounds most certain, so the calibrator is the part that would make confidence checkable. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the calibration literature, notably Guo’s 2017 study showing that modern deep networks are often overconfident, and on the conformal-prediction methods that give guarantees without assuming the model is right. The calibrator form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Uncertainty Calibrator is a design-study proposal for the component that would keep a general system’s confidence honest: measuring the gap between stated confidence and observed accuracy, and adjusting the scores until they match. A system whose confidence is miscalibrated is dangerous precisely where it sounds most certain, so the calibrator is the part that would make confidence checkable. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system’s confidence be kept honest, so that when it says it is 80 percent sure, it is right about 80 percent of the time?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: produce a confidence score with every prediction. Step 2: plot the calibration curve, comparing stated confidence with observed accuracy. Step 3: recalibrate, adjusting the scores until they match. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted confidence scores against five observed accuracy scores and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.4; mse: 2.2; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Uncertainty Calibrator proposes confidence scoring, a calibration curve, and recalibration; the demo’s error check gives MSE 2.2. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.4; mse: 2.2; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this calibrator as a design template for honest confidence, and check calibration on the deployment population. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A calibrator on paper is easy to fake: a system can be well calibrated on average and wildly wrong on the cases that matter, and confidence scores can be tuned to a benchmark while failing in deployment. The honest statement names the population the calibration was measured on, because calibration does not travel. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted confidence scores against five observed accuracy scores give a mean squared error of 2.2, a mean absolute error of 1.4, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a built calibrator. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: calibrator = confidence + curve + recalibration. An uncertainty calibrator keeps a system’s confidence honest by measuring and adjusting it.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the calibration literature: the cited sources document miscalibration and its fixes. The calibrator claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a calibration component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: uncertainty calibrator = confidence score + calibration curve + recalibration. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the calibration literature.

The design builds on the calibration literature, notably Guo’s 2017 study showing that modern deep networks are often overconfident, and on the conformal-prediction methods that give guarantees without assuming the model is right. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(85, 87), (72, 71), (90, 89), (66, 68), (79, 78)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureAgiUncertaintyCalibratorProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(85, 87), (72, 71), (90, 89), (66, 68), (79, 78)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
            mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
            mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
            max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureAgiUncertaintyCalibratorProgram().run())

Signature AGI Deliberation Scheduler JAH-AGIIS-000043 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Deliberation Scheduler is a design-study proposal for the component that would decide how much thinking a problem deserves: when to answer fast from intuition, when to slow down and deliberate, and when to stop thinking and act. The scheduler weighs the expected gain from more deliberation against the cost of the time spent, and it applies the stopping rule that bounded rationality requires. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on Herbert Simon’s bounded rationality, which argued that real agents satisfice under limits, and on the metareasoning literature, which studies reasoning about how much reasoning to do. The scheduler form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Deliberation Scheduler is a design-study proposal for the component that would decide how much thinking a problem deserves: when to answer fast from intuition, when to slow down and deliberate, and when to stop thinking and act. The scheduler weighs the expected gain from more deliberation against the cost of the time spent, and it applies the stopping rule that bounded rationality requires. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system decide how much thinking a problem deserves, and when to stop thinking and act?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: estimate the value of more thinking against its cost. Step 2: use an anytime method, so an answer is always available. Step 3: stop when the expected gain from more deliberation falls below its cost. Step 4: run the demo, which computes Hoeffding 95 percent error bounds at four trial counts, as a worked example of how uncertainty shrinks with more samples. The demo's computed check reads: epsilon_200: 0.096; epsilon_2000: 0.0304; epsilon_50: 0.1921; epsilon_800: 0.048; n_values: 4. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_values": [50, 200, 800, 2000]}.

The Answer

The Deliberation Scheduler proposes a value-of-thinking rule with an anytime method and a stopping rule; the demo’s Hoeffding bounds show uncertainty shrinking with samples. Computed check: epsilon_200: 0.096; epsilon_2000: 0.0304; epsilon_50: 0.1921; epsilon_800: 0.048; n_values: 4.

When It Applies

Use this scheduler as a design template for thinking under limits, and test the stopping rule under real deadlines. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A scheduler on paper assumes the value of thinking can be estimated, and real estimates are guesses: a system may stop too early on a problem that needed one more step, or deliberate too long on a problem that needed action. The stopping rule is the whole skill, and it is rarely tested under real time pressure. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo computes Hoeffding bounds at trial counts 50, 200, 800, and 2000: epsilon 0.1921, 0.096, 0.048, and 0.0304. The bound halves each time the sample count quadruples, which is the shape of statistical confidence. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: scheduler = value of thinking - cost of time. A deliberation scheduler decides how much thinking a problem deserves and when to stop and act.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the bounded-rationality literature: the cited sources document satisficing under constraints. The scheduler claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a deliberation component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: deliberation scheduler = value estimate + anytime method + stopping rule. The demo computes Hoeffding bounds; the sources confirm the bounded-rationality lineage.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
ns = [50, 200, 800, 2000]
def compute():
    out = {}
    for n in ns:
        out["epsilon_%d" % n] = round(math.sqrt(math.log(40) / (2 * n)), 4)
    out["n_values"] = len(ns)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
