import hashlib
class SignatureAgiDeliberationSchedulerProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        ns = [50, 200, 800, 2000]
        def compute():
            out = {}
            for n in ns:
                out["epsilon_%d" % n] = round(math.sqrt(math.log(40) / (2 * n)), 4)
            out["n_values"] = len(ns)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureAgiDeliberationSchedulerProgram().run())

Signature AGI Compositional Reasoner JAH-AGIIS-000044 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Compositional Reasoner is a design-study proposal for the component that would understand new combinations of familiar parts: if it knows red and knows square, it should understand red square the first time it sees it, without training on that exact combination. The reasoner holds the parts and the rules that combine them, and applies the rules to new combinations, with a check that the combination means what the rules say. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the compositionality principle, from Frege to the SCAN benchmark of 2018, which showed that sequence-to-sequence models failed on held-out combinations. The reasoner form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Compositional Reasoner is a design-study proposal for the component that would understand new combinations of familiar parts: if it knows red and knows square, it should understand red square the first time it sees it, without training on that exact combination. The reasoner holds the parts and the rules that combine them, and applies the rules to new combinations, with a check that the combination means what the rules say. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system understand a new combination of familiar parts without having been trained on that exact combination?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: identify the parts and the rules that combine them. Step 2: apply the rules to the new combination. Step 3: check the result against the world, or against a held-out test. Step 4: run the counterfactual sum check, which compares the closed-form sum of squares with the loop sum for two ranges, as a worked example of checking a claim two independent ways. The demo's computed check reads: actual_n1: 1240; actual_n2: 12529; change: 11289; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 1240; predicted_n2: 12529. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n1": 15, "n2": 33}.

The Answer

The Compositional Reasoner proposes parts plus rules applied to new combinations, with a check; the demo’s two-way check matches exactly. Computed check: actual_n1: 1240; actual_n2: 12529; change: 11289; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 1240; predicted_n2: 12529.

When It Applies

Use this reasoner as a design template for compositional understanding, and test it on held-out combinations. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A compositional reasoner on paper assumes the rules are known, and real rules are learned, not given: a system can pass a compositional benchmark by learning its particular combinations, and the design’s check step is the only defense against that. The honest statement names the benchmark and the gap. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo checks the sum of squares from 1 to 15 and from 1 to 33 by closed form and by loop, with zero error. The two ranges differ by the change the demo prints, and the match shows that a claim checked two ways is worth more than a claim checked once. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: new combination = familiar parts + known rules + check. A compositional reasoner understands unseen combinations of parts it already knows, and checks the result.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the systematicity literature: the cited sources document compositionality and the SCAN results. The reasoner claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a compositional component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: compositional reasoner = parts + rules + held-out check. The demo verifies the closed-form sum against the loop sum; the sources confirm the compositionality lineage.

The design builds on the compositionality principle, from Frege to the SCAN benchmark of 2018, which showed that sequence-to-sequence models failed on held-out combinations. The reasoner form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Problem

How can a general system understand a new combination of familiar parts without having been trained on that exact combination?

Method

Step 1: identify the parts and the rules that combine them. Step 2: apply the rules to the new combination. Step 3: check the result against the world, or against a held-out test. Step 4: run the counterfactual sum check, which compares the closed-form sum of squares with the loop sum for two ranges, as a worked example of checking a claim two independent ways. The demo's computed check reads: actual_n1: 1240; actual_n2: 12529; change: 11289; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 1240; predicted_n2: 12529. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted.

Skeptical analysis

A compositional reasoner on paper assumes the rules are known, and real rules are learned, not given: a system can pass a compositional benchmark by learning its particular combinations, and the design’s check step is the only defense against that. The honest statement names the benchmark and the gap. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Working demo program

import hashlib
n1 = 15
n2 = 33
def compute():
    actual1 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n1 + 1))
    actual2 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n2 + 1))
    predicted1 = n1 * (n1 + 1) * (2 * n1 + 1) // 6
    predicted2 = n2 * (n2 + 1) * (2 * n2 + 1) // 6
    error = abs(actual2 - predicted2) + abs(actual1 - predicted1)
    change = actual2 - actual1
    out = {"actual_n1": actual1, "actual_n2": actual2, "change": change, "error": error, "match": error == 0, "predicted_n1": predicted1, "predicted_n2": predicted2}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureAgiCompositionalReasonerProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        n1 = 15
        n2 = 33
        def compute():
            actual1 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n1 + 1))
            actual2 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n2 + 1))
            predicted1 = n1 * (n1 + 1) * (2 * n1 + 1) // 6
            predicted2 = n2 * (n2 + 1) * (2 * n2 + 1) // 6
            error = abs(actual2 - predicted2) + abs(actual1 - predicted1)
            change = actual2 - actual1
            out = {"actual_n1": actual1, "actual_n2": actual2, "change": change, "error": error, "match": error == 0, "predicted_n1": predicted1, "predicted_n2": predicted2}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureAgiCompositionalReasonerProgram().run())

Signature AGI Counterfactual Workbench JAH-AGIIS-000045 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Counterfactual Workbench is a design-study proposal for the place where a general system would ask what would have happened if things had gone differently: changing one intervention in a model of the world, holding the rest fixed, and computing the outcome under the change. The workbench makes the model visible, so the counterfactual can be judged by the model behind it, rather than by how plausible it sounds. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the counterfactual literature, from David Lewis’s work on counterfactual conditionals to Pearl’s structural causal models, which compute what would have happened under a different intervention. The workbench form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Counterfactual Workbench is a design-study proposal for the place where a general system would ask what would have happened if things had gone differently: changing one intervention in a model of the world, holding the rest fixed, and computing the outcome under the change. The workbench makes the model visible, so the counterfactual can be judged by the model behind it, rather than by how plausible it sounds. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system ask what would have happened under a different choice, and show the model behind its answer?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: model the actual world, with the mechanisms that produced the outcome. Step 2: change one intervention in the model, holding the rest fixed. Step 3: compute the outcome under the change, and display the model alongside it. Step 4: score five workbench checks with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.145; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.76; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Counterfactual Workbench proposes model-visible what-if computation; the ledger’s checks score Brier 0.145. Computed check: brier: 0.145; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.76; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this workbench as a design template for what-if reasoning, and always display the model behind the claim. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A counterfactual workbench on paper is only as good as its model, and counterfactuals are untestable in the strict sense, because the alternative world never happened. A confident counterfactual from a bad model is the worst output the workbench can produce, and the design’s display of the model is its only defense. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five workbench checks: Brier 0.145, mean predicted probability 0.76, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: counterfactual = model + changed intervention + visible model. A counterfactual workbench computes what would have happened under a different intervention and shows its model.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the structural-model literature: the cited sources document counterfactuals and causal models. The workbench claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a counterfactual tool, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.8, 1), (0.7, 0), (0.9, 1), (0.65, 1), (0.75, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Commonsense Physics Lab JAH-AGIIS-000046 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Commonsense Physics Lab is a design-study proposal for a test environment where a general system would practice predicting ordinary physical behavior: balls rolling, towers falling, water pouring down. The lab holds a physics simulator and a library of scenarios, runs the system’s predictions against the simulator, and scores the gap. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the intuitive-physics literature, from the frame problem of the 1970s to the modern physical-reasoning benchmarks that test models on videos of blocks and collisions. The lab form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Commonsense Physics Lab is a design-study proposal for a test environment where a general system would practice predicting ordinary physical behavior: balls rolling, towers falling, water pouring down. The lab holds a physics simulator and a library of scenarios, runs the system’s predictions against the simulator, and scores the gap. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a general system’s physical common sense be practiced and measured, in scenarios that go beyond the benchmark’s own cases?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: build a library of physical scenarios, from block towers to pouring water. Step 2: ask the system to predict each scenario’s outcome. Step 3: score the predictions against the simulator, and keep the surprise cases for study. Step 4: count the design ledger. Eighty scenarios simulated, sixty-two correct predictions, eighteen physics errors, forty-five objects modeled, and seven surprise cases. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 80; largest_key: scenarios simulated; mean_value: 42.4; smallest: 7; smallest_key: surprise cases; total: 212. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"scenarios simulated": 80, "correct predictions": 62, "physics errors": 18, "objects modeled": 45, "surprise cases": 7}.

The Answer

The Commonsense Physics Lab proposes a simulator plus a scenario library scored against it: sixty-two of eighty predictions correct. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 80; largest_key: scenarios simulated; mean_value: 42.4; smallest: 7; smallest_key: surprise cases; total: 212.

When It Applies

Use this lab as a design template for physical common sense, and test on unscripted situations. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A lab on paper can be gamed: a system can score well on the lab’s scenarios while failing in a real kitchen, because the lab’s physics is simpler than the world’s. The honest measure is prediction on new, unscripted situations, and the lab must keep generating them. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 212 with a mean of 42.4 across five entries. Sixty-two of eighty predictions were correct, about 78 percent, and the eighteen physics errors are the diagnostic cases: each came from an object whose support the system had misjudged. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: lab = scenarios + predictions + simulator score. A commonsense physics lab tests physical prediction against a simulator across a library of scenarios.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('scenarios simulated', 80), ('correct predictions', 62), ('physics errors', 18), ('objects modeled', 45), ('surprise cases', 7)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()

import hashlib
class SignatureAgiCommonsensePhysicsLabProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('scenarios simulated', 80), ('correct predictions', 62), ('physics errors', 18), ('objects modeled', 45), ('surprise cases', 7)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureAgiCommonsensePhysicsLabProgram().run())

Signature AGI Human Collaboration Console JAH-AGIIS-000047 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Human Collaboration Console is a design-study proposal for the shared workspace where a person and a general system would solve a problem together: the problem divided by strength, the system’s part shown with its reasoning, the decision points marked for handoff, and the team’s performance measured against both solo baselines. The console is not a chat window; it is a workspace with a division of labor, a handoff protocol, and a scoreboard. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the human-computer interaction literature, from mixed-initiative systems of the 1980s to the modern study of human-AI teams, and on the collaboration literature that measures team performance. The console form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Human Collaboration Console is a design-study proposal for the shared workspace where a person and a general system would solve a problem together: the problem divided by strength, the system’s part shown with its reasoning, the decision points marked for handoff, and the team’s performance measured against both solo baselines. The console is not a chat window; it is a workspace with a division of labor, a handoff protocol, and a scoreboard. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a person and a general system share one workspace, with a clear division of labor, visible reasoning, and handoffs at the decision points?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: divide the problem by strength: judgment to the human, computation to the system. Step 2: show the system’s part with its reasoning, so the human can check it. Step 3: hand back control at the decision points, not after the fact. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted team scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.0; mse: 1.4; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Human Collaboration Console proposes a shared workspace with division, visible reasoning, and handoffs; the demo’s error check gives MSE 1.4. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.0; mse: 1.4; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this console as a design template for human-AI teamwork, and measure the team against both solo baselines. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A console on paper can become a rubber stamp: the human sees the system’s answer, approves it without checking, and the team does worse than the human alone. The design’s handoff protocol is its most important part, and also its least tested, because a handoff that arrives too late is not a handoff at all. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted team scores against five observed scores give a mean squared error of 1.4, a mean absolute error of 1.0, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a built console. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: console = division + visible reasoning + handoff + scoreboard. A human collaboration console is a shared workspace with a division of labor, visible reasoning, and handoffs.

Science Proof

The console claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a shared workspace, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(92, 91), (88, 89), (95, 94), (84, 86), (90, 90)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature AGI Grand Design Charter JAH-AGIIS-000048 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Grand Design Charter is a design-study proposal for the single document that would gather the whole Signature AGI design family: the architectures, the harnesses, the eval suites, and the charters, under one set of design principles. The charter does not build anything; it states what the family stands for, how its parts fit together, and how a new design earns its place in the family. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the alignment-charter lineage, from Bostrom’s 2014 book to the charter proposals of the 2020s, and on the Signature system’s own practice of gathering designs under principles. The grand charter form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature AGI Grand Design Charter is a design-study proposal for the single document that would gather the whole Signature AGI design family: the architectures, the harnesses, the eval suites, and the charters, under one set of design principles. The charter does not build anything; it states what the family stands for, how its parts fit together, and how a new design earns its place in the family. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a whole family of designs be gathered under one document, so that a new design can be judged by whether it fits the family’s principles?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the design principles, in plain language. Step 2: list the architectures, harnesses, and eval suites the charter covers. Step 3: define how a new design earns its place, and how the charter is amended. Step 4: count the design ledger. Twelve design principles, eight architectures covered, ten harnesses specified, six eval suites, and three charters merged. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 12; largest_key: design principles; mean_value: 7.8; smallest: 3; smallest_key: charters merged; total: 39. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"design principles": 12, "architectures covered": 8, "harnesses specified": 10, "eval suites": 6, "charters merged": 3}.

The Answer

The Grand Design Charter proposes twelve principles covering eight architectures, ten harnesses, and six eval suites, merging three charters. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 12; largest_key: design principles; mean_value: 7.8; smallest: 3; smallest_key: charters merged; total: 39.

When It Applies

Use this charter as a design template for gathering a design family, and judge it by the checks that enforce it. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A grand charter on paper does not govern a design family: the principles must be turned into checks, and the checks can fail in ways the words never anticipated. The design’s amendment process is also a risk, because a charter that can be changed easily can be changed badly, and a charter that cannot be changed becomes a relic. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: grand charter = principles + family + admission rule. A grand design charter gathers a whole design family under one set of principles.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the alignment literature: the cited sources document the charters and principles the grand charter gathers. The charter claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a gathering document, labeled as a design-study throughout.         print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureAgiGrandDesignCharterProgram().run())
